研 究  報 告                
南亞科(2408.TT) 
產業：電子–半導體 
價格與⻑約重議價推升獲利，AI 需求⽀撐中⻑期成⻑  
 投資評價與建議 
受惠與記憶體供不應求持續，目前⻑約覆蓋率約50%～70%之間，合約期限從
季度到⻑達3年不等，其中，3年期合約佔總產出20%以上，有助營運能見度提
升；另隨著AI應用占比提升，有助降低記憶體景氣循環影響。 
 重點評論分析 
3Q26反應⻑期合約重新簽訂之調整，ASP季增超過60%： 3Q26營收季增
61.9%至1,336.5億元，年增611.7%，受惠平均售價季增60+%，其⻑約貢獻超
過⼀半以上；位元出貨量季減中個位數百分比，⽑利率79.5%攀升至85.7%，
營業淨利率80%，季增6.3ppts，營業費用75.88億元，費用增加主因當期及未
來的員工薪酬增加，稅後淨利876.27億元，稅後EPS 25.4元，優於預期。 
4Q26供不應求延續，預期ASP持續上揚、出貨持平：展望4Q26，公司預期市
場供不應求持續，ASP持續向上，惟在位元出貨量，由於目前庫存處於低檔，
預期未來數個季度的位元出貨量將保持平穩，待4Q27新廠開始投產，預估
4Q26營收季增21.7%至1,626.7億元，⽑利率86.6%，稅後31.29元。 
随新廠產能開出，目標AI基礎設施逐步提升：新廠規劃1Q27裝機，因無大
幅增加產出的空間，加上庫存處於低水位，預期2027年初出貨量持平。南
亞科目前AI基礎設施相關營收占比已超過20%，含蓋通訊、儲存、系統管
理、eSSD以及RDIMM模組等，2030年以前目標提升至40～50%。 
 投資風險 
AI基礎建設投資放緩、客製化AI/WoW產品進度不如預期

### Page 2

2 
| 營運概況  
3Q26 反應⻑期合約重新簽訂之調整 ， ASP 季增超過 60% ：3Q26 營收季增 61.9%至 1,336.5 億元 ， 年增 611.7% ，
受惠平均售價季增 60+%，其⻑約貢獻超過⼀半以上；位元出貨量季減中個位數百分比，⽑利率 79%攀升至 85.7%，
營業淨利 1,068.9 億元，營業淨利率 80%，季增 6.3ppts，營業費用 75.88 億元，費用增加主因當期及未來的員
工薪酬增加 ， 稅後淨利 876.27 億元 ， 淨利率 65.6% ， 稅後 EPS 25.4 元 ， 優於預期 。 產品結構部份 ， DDR4/LPDDR4
佔比逾 60%，DDR5 約佔 10%；此外，AI 基礎設施(含伺服器、網路、儲存及系統管理)占營收超過 20%。 
4Q26 供不應求延續，預期 ASP 持續上揚、出貨持平：展望 4Q26，公司預期市場供不應求持續，ASP 持續向上，
惟在位元出貨量，由於目前庫存處於低檔，預期未來數個季度的位元出貨量將保持平穩，直到 4Q27 新廠開始投
產，預估 4Q26 營收季增 21.7%至 1,626.7 億元，⽑利率 86.6%，稅後 31.29 元。此外，就供需市況部份，隨著 AI
運算主導 DRAM 需求成⻑ ， 延伸至網路 、 儲存及系統管理等應用 ， 推升高容量 、 高頻寬及客製化記憶體架構需求 ，
而傳統應用因受限於供給不足加上報價調漲，轉而降低規格、延⻑⽣命週期來因應；另供給則因新廠建置、設備
交期、製程導入，均需要較⻑的前置時間，預期供不應求狀況在 2027 年至 2028 年前難以解決。 
随新廠產能開出，目標 AI 基礎設施應用占比逐步提升：產能規劃方面，新廠規劃 1Q27 裝機，因無大幅增加產出
的空間，再加上庫存消耗後，正處低水位，預期 2027 年初出貨量持平，隨著 4Q27 新廠開始投產後有望逐步提
升，目標 2028 年達每月投片量３萬片，2030 年年底達到每月投片量５萬片。此外，第三代 10 奈米級製程(1C)
預計 1Q27 開始客戶驗證，並同步開發第四代(1D)、第五代(1E)製程及 EUV，均按計畫推進。另規劃於屏東科學
園區建置 3D 晶圓封裝與測試廠，預計 2028 年年底前導入矽穿孔(TSV)、晶圓堆疊等 3D IC 先進技術，未來擴建
皆以客戶⻑期合約的 AI 專案為目標。南亞科目前 AI 基礎設施相關營收占比已超過 20%，含蓋通訊、儲存、系統
管理、eSSD 以及 RDIMM 模組等，2030 年以前目標提升至 40～50%；此外，目前⻑約覆蓋率約 50%～70%之
間，合約期限從季度到⻑達 3 年不等，其中，3 年期合約佔總產出 20%以上，有助營運能見度提升；另與客戶合
作 AI 相關晶圓對晶圓技術 ASIC 專案，應用範圍涵蓋雲端 AI 運算、邊緣運算及物理 AI，已有營收貢獻，雖然目
前規模不大，後續業務發展值得關注。預估 2027 年營收 7,378.76 億元，⽑利率 87.1%，稅後 EPS 141.9 元，維
持「買進」之投資建議，目標價 710 元(3.8 倍 PBR*2027BVPS(F))。

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: TSMC continued delivering “beat-n-raise” prints for its 
quarterly results and guidance on 15 October. While we raise our forecasts 
to factor in its better topline and pricing trajectory yet muted margin 
expectation on an incremental basis, we see likely lack of near-term 
passion from investors on limited positive surprises from its results call this 
time. That said, we believe AI demand remains strong and sustainable to 
support our Buy (1) call on the stock, though patience may be necessary 
for investors amid the current jittery environment.

What’s the impact: Results intact, guidance mixed. Despite 3Q26 
topline of USD46.7bn topping guidance (USD44.6-45.8bn) at TSMC, its 
3Q26 EPS came in at TWD29.4, only marginally beating our/Bloomberg 
consensus estimates on lower opex and higher non-operating gains from 
long-term investments more than offsetting lower gross margin associated 
with higher depreciation costs on dilutive N2 ramp (table on p.2). Guidance 
calls for higher topline for 4Q26 with midpoint of the range 6% above our 
previous forecast, thanks to continued AI strength as customers continue to 
increase demand. Yet margins at the operating level looked muted without 
further operating leverage being expected by the street, due to the margin-
dilutive N2 ramp. We raise our 2026-28E forecasts by 3-10% to factor in 
the better topline trajectory, plus our view of better pricing power.

Lack of near-term passion? Apart from the upbeat topline but muted 
margin guidance on an incremental basis, we left the results call with 
limited positive surprises this time which may not drive any passion for 
investors in the near term to catalyse the stock. That said, fundamentals 
remain strong since AI demand continues to mushroom from accelerators 
to CPU and other companion chips, as language models transform from 
generative to agentic for AI assistance. TSMC implied upside risk to its 
long-term AI accelerator revenue CAGR of mid-to-high-50% over 2024-29 
and, as flagged in our Asian Foundry report, we saw upside risk to TSMC’s 
pricing power for 2027, given the still meaningful supply constraint where 
management reiterated its strong conviction in this multi-year AI megatrend 
and active capacity build aimed to narrow the supply/demand gap.

What we recommend: We raise our 12M TP for TSMC to TWD3,200 
(previous: TWD3,000), based on our finetuned ROE-adjusted PBR target of 
7.5x one-year forward BVPS forecast (previous: 7.7x). We reiterate our Buy 
(1) call with key downside risks of “silicon inflation” and geopolitical 
overhangs leading to global inflation and AI capex cut.

How we differ: Our 2028E EPS is 11% below the Bloomberg consensus 
likely on our view calling for a soft-landing in the chip cycle.

15 October 2026

Lack of passion despite strong AI?

➢ Another “beat-n-raise” in quarterly prints but likely … 
➢ … short of catalysts on limited positive surprise this time 
➢ Reaffirming our Buy (1) call with a higher 12M TP of TWD3,200

Company profile 
Incorporated in Taiwan in 1987, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMC) is the world’s largest 
complementary metal oxide semiconductor (CMOS) foundry in revenue terms. TSMC offers foundry 
services such as wafer masking, fabrication, probing, packaging and testing, to a high variety of 
customers including fabless chipmakers and IDMs. Its manufacturing fabs are located in Taiwan, China, 
the US and Singapore.

### Page 5

5

Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (2330 TT): 15 October 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 15 Oct 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 1
TSMC's board of directors (BoD) has 10 members, including 6 independent directors
representing 60% of total directors. The background of TSMC's BoD is diversified, with members
from industries, academia, and law. Also, its board members include representatives from not
only Taiwan but also Europe and the US, bringing their experience in operating international
businesses to the board.
We believe the high percentage of independent directors and a diverse mix of expertise from the
board members strengthen the functionality of its BoD, as evidenced by TSMC's leading position
in the foundry market.
Capital management 1
TSMC pays its dividend on a quarterly basis. In the past 5 years, it has managed its dividend
payout well, as evidenced by its dividend in dollar terms, where it has never lowered its dividend.
As the leader in advanced technology nodes, TSMC has kept expanding its capacity for
advanced nodes; as such, we expect its capital intensity to increase to the level near or above
40% over 2025-26 (vs. its long-term target of mid-30%). However, we believe TSMC will at least
sustain its cash dividend payout in dollar terms over 2025-26E. We see its balance between
investment and dividend as appropriate.
Related party &
transaction 1
TSMC's sales to related parties and purchases from related parties remain immaterial as a
percentage of total revenue and cost of goods sold in 2026. We view these levels as insignificant
and therefore see limited risk to TSMC's earnings, cash flow generation, or corporate
governance.
S Supply chainmanagement 1
As the leader in the global foundry industry, TSMC approaches supply chain management by
focusing on 2 main strategies: sustainability risk management and local supply chain
optimisation. It requires all its suppliers to comply with its "Code with Ethics and Business
Conduct", and to follow regulations on human rights and conflict-free minerals.
In 2019, 100% of its tier-1 suppliers signed the "Supplier Code of Conduct" and self-assessment
questionnaire on sustainable management. Also, TSMC's raw materials purchased are 100%
Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) conflict-free. It also requires its critical suppliers to
report on the status of sustainability management in their critical upstream supply chains.
Through these measures, we see TSMC reducing its supply chain risk.
E Water & wastewatermanagement 2
TSMC applies internal and external company resources to develop water reclaimation
technology, implements water conservation measures and uses reeclaimed water in the
manufacturing process. For example, on September 19, 2022, TSMC Tainan Science Park
Reclaimed Water Plant started supplying reclaimed water. In 2025, TSMC aims to replace 5% of
water resources with reclaimed water, and continue to cooperate with the government to
complete the 2nd water reclamation plan in Anping, Tainan. In 2030, TSMC targets to replace >
60% of water resources with reclaimed water.
E
Waste & hazardous
materials
management
1
TSMC's waste recycling rate reached 96% in 2022 for 8 years in a row. Also, the percentage of
waste sent to landfills was less than 1% for 13 years in a row in 2022.
TSMC achieved reducing its outsourced unit waste disposal (kg/12" equivalent wafer mask
layer) to 0.99 in 2022. Furthermore, TSMC has set a goal to reduce its outsourced unit waste
disposal per wafer to no more than 0.5 in 2030.
E GHG emissions 1
TSMC has continued its efforts to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions to drive low-carbon
manufacturing. In 2022, TSMC achieved net zero emissions of direct and indirect (from
purchased energy) GHG Emissions in overseas factories, and 100% use of
carbon-neutral natural gas in Taiwan fabs. It has set a target to achieve Net Zero Emissions by
2050.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

证券投资咨询业务证书编号：S1300516090001

9 月和三季度经济数据点评

内需出现向好迹象   
9 月经济数据中，固投累计同比增速和社零当月同比增速均好于万得一致预期，工业
增加值当月同比增速符合万得一致预期且维持在相对较高水平。 
◼ 2026 年三季度 GDP 实际同比增速 4.5%，三季度名义 GDP 同比增速 6.0%；前三
季度实际 GDP 累计同比增长 4.6%，名义 GDP 累计同比增长 5.6%。 
◼ 9 月工业增加值同比增长 5.5%。1-9 月采矿业工业增加值累计同比增速 2.0%，制
造业累计同比增速 5.7%，公用事业累计同比增速 5.4%，高技术产业累计同比增
速 14.3%。 
◼ 9 月社零同比增长 1.1%， 其中， 除汽车以外的消费品零售额同比增长3.9%。 限额
以上商品消费中，算力消费品依旧主导社零，金价下跌推动金银珠宝消费，房地
产后周期消费短期未见好转。 
◼ 1-9 月固定资产投资累计同比增速下降 7.2%，民间固定资产投资累计同比下降
10.4%， 扣除房地产开发投资，1-9 月全国固定资产投资下降 4.2%， 民间投资下降
6.7%。 分主要行业看， 制造业投资1-9 月累计同比下降 2.1%， 基建投资累计同比
下降 6.1%，地产投资累计同比下降 20.6%。 
◼ 1-9 月住宅投资增速下降 20.5%， 商品房销售面积同比下降13.2%， 商品房销售额
累计同比下降 13.9%。2026 年 9 月，70 个大中城市新建商品住宅价格指数同比下
降 3.0%，较 8 月上升 0.3 个百分点，二手住宅价格指数下降 4.8%，较 8 月上升
0.3 个百分点。 
◼ 2026 年前三季度全国居民人均可支配收入 34230 元，同比增长 5.3%，居民人均
消费支出 22354 元，同比增长 2.7%。2026 年前三季度居民边际消费倾向较上半
年小幅上升，但整体来看居民消费意愿较弱，边际消费倾向和增量收入边际消费
倾向对比 2025 年同期均有所下降，特别是增量收入边际消费倾向降幅较大。 
◼ 三季度经济增速超预期，全年稳增长压力下降。三季度实际 GDP 同比增长 4.5%
超出市场预期，带动前三季度实际 GDP 累计同比增速达到 4.6%，略高于全年增
速目标区间下限，加之去年四季度经济增长基数偏低，一定程度上缓解了短期稳
增长的压力。从 9 月经济数据看，稳增长的政策起到了一定效果：工业增加值同
比增速符合市场预期，同时维持在较高增速上；社零同比增速较 8 月明显上升，
表明内需仍有平稳释放的潜力；固定资产投资累计同比降幅持平于 1-8 月，但从
我们计算的当月增速看， 基建投资和制造业投资增速均较8 月出现较大幅度好转。
从季节规律看，四季度经济增长仍有支撑：一是生产端补库存，可能驱动工业增
加值同比增速维持在当前的增速水平，考虑到 PPI 同比增速走高和 9 月出口交货
值增速走高，价格因素和外需可能成为四季度生产端超预期的推动力；二是四季
度是传统消费旺季，从社零环比增速看，在 9 月环比高增长的基础上，四季度的
三个月在电商促销和春节前消费等因素推动下，环比仍能维持较强的正增长；三
是四季度也是海外消费旺季，进出口或依赖我国生产端优势，持续受益于外需拉
动。 
◼ 经济向“新”带动政策求“变”。 8 月底至今，从稳增长的角度出发，政策做了
多方面调整：一是国家发改委等部门召开全国投资工作推进会议，部署七项稳投
资工作，二是住建部等三部门推进商品住房销售制度改革，稳定地产预期，三是
证监会发文支持构建房地产发展新模式，四是央行调整完善几项货币政策工具稳
增长， 五是财政部安排使用地方政府债务结存限额5500 亿元， 六是中共中央、 国
务院印发《关于发展新质生产力的意见》，继续大力支持经济转型升级等等。我
们认为虽然 2026 年年内稳增长的压力减轻， 但从中长期看， 经济增长的挑战依然
很大： 从全球范围内看， 包括但不限于AI 技术发展对生产关系的冲击、 地缘局势
变化对能源和通胀的影响、 经济增长K 型分化在行业之间和国别之间带来的发展
不均衡、各经济体面临的增长挑战与财政压力、发达国家货币政策收缩与金融条
件收紧对全球资本市场的外溢性影响等等；从国内看，我们面临的挑战依然是以
维持经济向新向优趋势和供强需弱的现实矛盾为主。我们认为未来一段时间内，
我国宏观经济的主要表现或仍是增速缓步下台阶，因此积极的财政政策和适度宽
松的货币政策发力重点或更多聚焦于托底民生和实现社会公平，其次是发展先进
生产力，然后才是稳增长。尽管国内经济看起来只是“平稳”，但我们认为相较
于其他经济体，我国的比较优势依然非常明显。 
◼ 风险提示：全球通胀二次上行；欧美经济超预期回落；国际局势复杂化。

### Page 2

2026 年 10 月 19 日 9 月和三季度经济数据点评 2

2026 年三季度实际 GDP 同比增长 4.5%  
2026 年三季度 GDP 实际同比增速 4.5%，较2026 年二季度增速上升 0.2 个百分点； 三季度名义GDP
同比增速 6.0%，较 2026 年二季度增速上升 0.1 个百分点。前三季度实际 GDP 累计同比增长 4.6%，
名义 GDP 累计同比增长 5.6%。 
前三季度平减指数累计同比上升 0.99%,较上半年平减指数累计同比上升 0.29 个百分点。三季度平减
指数上升，主要还是受 PPI 同比增速处于较高水平的拉动。

图表 1. GDP 同比增速

资料来源：万得，中银证券

三季度第二产业和第三产业实际 GDP 当季同比增速均较二季度有所上升 。 
分产业看 ，2026 年三季度第一产业实际 GDP 当季同比增长 3.6%， 增速较二季度下降0.1 个百分点 ，
第二产业实际 GDP 当季同比增长 3.4%，增速较二季度上升 0.4 个百分点，第三产业实际 GDP 当季
同比增长 5.2%，增速较二季度上升 0.1 个百分点。 
从三次产业对 GDP 累计同比贡献率看，2026 年前三季度,第三产业的贡献率为 65.9%,较上半年下降
0.2 个百分点，其次是第二产业的贡献率 29.5%,较上半年下降 0.7 个百分点，较上半年贡献率上升的
主要是第一产业，贡献率 4.6%，上升 0.9 个百分点；与 2023 年-2025 年三次产业的贡献率平均水平
相比，2026 年前三季度第三产业对 GDP 累计同比的贡献率偏高 4.0 个百分点，第二产业贡献率偏低
3.8 个百分点。

### Page 3

2026 年 10 月 19 日 9 月和三季度经济数据点评 3

工业增加值同比增速维持在较高水平 
9 月工业增加值同比增长 5.5%，较 8 月上升 0.3 个百分点，较去年同期下降 1.0 个百分点，基本持平
于万得一致预期的 5.52%。

图表 4. 工业增加值同比增速

资料来源：万得，中银证券

从环比增速来看，9 月工业增加值环比上升 0.61%，与 2016 年-2019 年期间同期平均水平 0.56%相比
偏高,略低于去年同期的环比增速 0.65%，9 月工业增加值环比较 8 月上行,符合季节性规律。 
从行业分类来看，1-9 月采矿业工业增加值累计同比增速 2.0%，制造业累计同比增速 5.7%，公用事
业累计同比增速 5.4%， 高技术产业累计同比增速14.3%。 从企业类型来看 ，1-9 月国企工业增加值同
比增速 3.8%，股份制企业同比增速 5.8%，外企工业增加值同比增速 3.2%，私企工业增加值同比增
速 4.3%。与 1-8 月相比，1-9 月累计同比增速上升的是公共事业、高技术产业和外企,下降的是采矿
业和私企。9 月当月工业增加值同比增速相较 8 月，各行业和企业类型中工业增加值同比增速上升
的是采矿业、公共事业、国企、股份制、外企和私企，下降的是高技术产业和制造业 。 
1-9 月，装备制造业增加值同比增长 10.2%，高技术制造业增加值增长 14.3%，分别快于全部规模以
上工业增加值 4.9 和 9.0 个百分点；分产品看，3D 打印设备、锂离子电池、工业机器人产品产量同
比分别增长 62.5%、39.5%、29.1%。

图表 5. 工业增加值环比增速  图表 6. 主要行业工业增加值当月同比增速

### Page 4

2026 年 10 月 19 日 9 月和三季度经济数据点评 4

9 月，采矿业和大宗周期品加工业当月工业增加值同比增速较 8 月增速相对较强，但受上游原材料
涨价影响，中游设备制造业工业增加值同比增速表现偏弱 。 
采矿业方面，1-9 月煤炭开采和洗选业增加值累计同比增长 0.2%，石油和天然气开采业增加值累计
同比增长 5.7%；从当月同比增速来看，较 8 月增速上升幅度均超过 2.0 个百分点。 
消费类制造业方面，1-9 月农副食品加工业增加值累计同比增长 4.6%，纺织业增加值累计同比增长
3.2%，医药制造业增加值累计同比增长 5.2%；从当月同比增速来看，较 8 月增速下降的是农副食品
加工业和医药制造业。 
大宗周期品加工业方面，1-9 月化学原料及化学制品制造业增加值累计同比增长 3.6%，非金属矿物
制品业增加值累计同比下降 2.9%， 黑色金属冶炼及压延加工业增加值累计同比增长1.7%， 有色金属
冶炼及压延加工业增加值累计同比下降 1.6%；从当月同比增速来看，较 8 月增速下降的仅有有色金
属，化学原料和非金属矿物当月工业增加值增速均较 8 月升幅超过 1.0 个百分点。 
中游设备制造业方面，1-9 月通用设备制造业增加值累计同比增长 8.2%，专用设备制造业增加值累
计同比增长 9.5%， 汽车制造业增加值累计同比增长7.3%， 电气机械及器材制造业增加值累计同比增
长 7.1%，计算机、通信和其他电子设备制造业增加值累计同比增长 15.8%；从当月同比增速来看，
较 8 月增速上升的仅有专用设备，汽车制造、电气设备和通用设备增速较 8 月降幅均超过 1.0 个百
分点。

2026 年 10 月 19 日 9 月和三季度经济数据点评 5

9 月外需延续较强走势，出口交货值同比大幅上升，产销率小幅上升。 
9 月规模以上工业企业产销率为 97.0%，较 8 月上升 0.2 个百分点，较去年同期上升 0.3 个百分点，
当月同比增速下降 0.2%；1-9 月规模以上工业企业产销率为 96.4%，较去年同期上升 0.5 个百分点，
累计同比增长 0.1 个百分点。 
9 月规模以上工业企业出口交货值同比增长 17.0%，较 8 月上升 5.9 个百分点，1-9 月规模以上工业
企业出口交货值累计同比增长 10.7%，较 1-8 月上升 0.7 个百分点。

社零增速明显上升 
9 月社零同比增长 1.1%，较8 月上升 0.7 个百分点 ， 其中 ， 除汽车以外的消费品零售额同比增长3.9%，
较 8 月上升 1.4 个百分点；1-9 月社零累计同比增长 1.1%，其中，服务消费累计同比增长 5.0%，较
1-8 月上升 0.1 个百分点。 
9 月社零环比增长 0.4%，虽然较历史同期平均水平明显偏低，但较去年同期环比增速上升 0.5 个百
分点。

1-9 月网上商品和服务零售额累计同比增长 4.4%，增速较 1-8 月下降 0.2 个百分点，其中实物商品网
上零售额累计同比增长 3.8%， 增速较1-8 月下降 0.5 个百分点 。1-9 月网上零售额累计占比为 40.9%，
较 2025 年同期上升 10.1 个百分点，较 1-8 月下降 0.2 个百分点。 
9 月商品消费同比增长 1.0%，较 8 月上升 0.7 个百分点，限额以上商品消费同比下降 2.5%，较 8 月
上升 1.5 个百分点；9 月餐饮消费同比增长 2.1%，较 8 月上升 1.0 个百分点，限额以上餐饮消费同比
增长 2.0%，较 8 月上升 2.7 个百分点。前三季度，在服务零售额中，通讯信息服务类、旅游咨询租
赁服务类、文体休闲服务类零售额较快增长。 
1-9 月限额以上企业消费品零售总额累计同比下降 1.5%， 限额以上企业商品消费累计同比下降1.8%,
弱于餐饮消费累计同比增速 1.3%。

9 月算力消费品依旧主导社零，金价下跌推动金银珠宝消费，房地产后周期消费短期未见好转 。 
从产品分类来看，9 月限额以上企业商品零售总额增速较高的细分行业包括通讯器材类（31.1%）、
烟酒类（13.8%）和文化办公用品类（7.9%），增速较低的细分行业包括汽车类（-21.2%）、金银珠
宝类 （-9.6%） 和建筑及装潢材料类 （-9.2%） ；与8 月相比，增速上升较多的有金银珠宝类、石油及
制品类和家具类，增速下降的仅有汽车类和家用电器。

1-9 月累计同比增速较高的品类有通讯器材类、 烟酒类和粮油食品类等， 累计同比降幅较大的品类有
汽车类、建筑及装潢材料类和石油及制品类等；累计同比增速较 1-8 月上升较多的有通讯器材类、
家用电器和音像器材类和石油及制品类等，累计同比增速较 1-8 月下降的有汽车类、金银珠宝类、
服装鞋帽针纺织品类和饮料类。

资料来源：万得，中银证券  资料来源：万得，中银证券

9 月制造业和基建当月投资增速较 8 月开始回升。 
从产业分类来看，第一产业累计同比下降 3.9%，第二产业累计同比下降 2.7%，第三产业累计同比下
降 10.0%； 从地区来看 ， 东部累计同比下降10.3%， 中部下降11.1%， 西部下降8.7%， 东北下降26.0%；
从企业类型来看，内资企业累计同比下降 7.0%，国有控股同比下降 3.2%，外商投资下降 6.7%。 
分主要行业看，制造业投资 1-9 月累计同比下降 2.1%，基建投资累计同比下降 6.1%，地产投资累计
同比下降 20.6%。从我们测算的当月同比增速看，9 月制造业投资同比增速下降 0.9%，较 8 月上升
5.1 个百分点，基建投资同比增速下降 10.4%，较 8 月上升 4.3 个百分点，房地产投资同比增速下降
27.2%，较 8 月下降 1.0 个百分点。 
1-9 月固投增速较高的一级分类行业包括采矿业（3.2%）、交运仓储（1.9%）和制造业（-2.1%），
固投增速较低的行业包括文体娱（-17.0%） 、教育（-15.3%）和卫生社会（-14.0%） 。制造业行业分
类下，固投增速较高的细分行业包括铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他运输设备制造业（15.8%） 、计算
机、通信和其他电子设备制造业（9.6%）和纺织业（4.2%），固投增速较低的行业包括食品制造业
（-10.3%）、金属制品业（-10.1%）和专用设备制造业（-8.9%）。 
与 1-8 月相比，一级分类行业中固投增速上行的行业是教育、制造业和公共事业，固投增速下行较
多的行业是文体娱、水利环境和农林牧渔业。制造业行业分类下，固投增速上行较多的行业是计算
机、通信和其他电子设备制造业、化学原料及化学制品制造业和电气机械及器材制造业，固投增速
下行较多的行业是纺织业、金属制品业和有色金属冶炼及压延加工业 。 
据统计局解读，1-9 月知识产权产品投资同比增长 9.3%，高技术产业投资同比增长 4.9%，其中信息
服务业，航空、航天器及设备制造业，电子及通信设备制造业投资分别增长 20.2%、12.5%、7.6%。

从居民收入来源来看，2026 年前三季度工资性收入同比增速 5.3%，经营净收入增速 6.6%，财产净

上半年下降 0.5 个百分点，经营净收入占比上升 0.4 个百分点，财产净收入占比下降 0.2 个百分点，

2026 年前三季度居民的边际消费倾向较上半年上升。2026 年前三季度居民人均消费支出 22354 元，
同比增长 2.7%，扣除价格因素，实际增速增长 2.7%，与 2026 年上半年相比，居民消费增速持平。
2026 年前三季度，居民消费八大分类中，同比增速较高的是其他用品及服务、生活用品及服务、教
育文化娱乐和食品烟酒，与 2026 年上半年相比，增速上升超过 1.0 个百分点的仅有医疗保健；2026
年前三季度 居民消费支出结构中占比较高的 还是食品烟酒（ 29.7%）、居住（ 21.3%）和交通通信
（14.3%），与 2026 年上半年相比，占比上升超过 1.0 个百分点的是教育文化娱乐，与 2025 年同期
相比，占比上升的是食品烟酒、生活用品及服务、教育文化娱乐和其他用品及服务 ，占比下降的是
居住、交通通信和医疗保健。2026 年前三季度居民边际消费倾向较上半年小幅上升，但整体来看居
民消费意愿较弱，边际消费倾向和增量收入边际消费倾向对比 2025 年同期均有所下降， 特别是增量
收入边际消费倾向降幅较大。

### Page 12

2026 年 10 月 19 日 9 月和三季度经济数据点评 12

结论：内需出现向好迹象 
三季度经济增速超预期，全年稳增长压力下降 。三季度实际 GDP 同比增长 4.5%超出市场预期，带
动前三季度实际 GDP 累计同比增速达到 4.6%，略高于全年增速目标区间下限，加之去年四季度经
济增长基数偏低，一定程度上缓解了短期稳增长的压力。从 9 月经济数据看，稳增长的政策起到了
一定效果：工业增加值同比增速符合市场预期，同时维持在较高增速上；社零同比增速较 8 月明显
上升，表明内需仍有平稳释放的潜力；固定资产投资累计同比降幅持平于 1-8 月，但从我们计算的
当月增速看，基建投资和制造业投资增速均较 8 月出现较大幅度好转。从季节规律看，四季度经济
增长仍有支撑：一是生产端补库存，可能驱动工业增加值同比增速维持在当前的增速水平，考虑到
PPI 同比增速走高和 9 月出口交货值增速走高，价格因素和外需可能成为四季度生产端超预期的推
动力；二是四季度是传统消费旺季，从社零环比增速看，在 9 月环比高增长的基础上，四季度的三
个月在电商促销和春节前消费等因素推动下，环比仍能 维持较强的正增长；三是四季度也是海外消
费旺季，进出口或依赖我国生产端优势，持续受益于外需拉动。 
经济向“新”带动政策求“变”。 8 月底至今，从稳增长的角度出发，政策做了多方面调整：一是
国家发改委等部门召开全国投资工作推进会议，部署七项稳投资 工作，二是住建部等三部门推进商
品住房销售制度改革，稳定地产预期，三是证监会发文支持构建房地产发展新模式，四是央行调整
完善几项货币政策工具稳增长， 五是财政部安排使用地方政府债务结存限额5500 亿元， 六是中共中
央、国务院印发《关于发展新质生产力的意见》，继续大力支持经济转型升级等等。我们认为虽然
2026 年年内稳增长的压力减轻，但从中长期看，经济增长的挑战依然很大：从全球范围内看，包括
但不限于 AI 技术发展对生产关系的冲击、地缘局势变化对能源和通胀的影响、经济增长 K 型分化
在行业之间和国别之间带来的发展不均衡、各经济体面临的增长挑战与财政压力、发达国家货币政
策收缩与金融条件收紧对全球资本市场的外溢性影响等等；从国内看，我们面临的挑战依然是以维
持经济向新向优趋势和供强需弱的现实矛盾为主。 我们认为未来一段时间内，我国宏观经济的主要
表现或仍是增速缓步下台阶，因此积极的财政政策和适度宽松的货币政策发力重点或更多聚焦于托
底民生和实现社会公平，其次是发展先进生产力，然后才是稳增长。 尽管国内经济看起来只是“平
稳”，但我们认为相较于其他经济体，我国的比较优势依然非常明显 。

风险提示：全球通胀二次上行；欧美经济 超预期回落；国际局势复杂化。

以报告发布日后公司股价/行业指数涨跌幅相对同期相关市场指数的涨跌幅的表现为基准：  
公司投资评级： 
买    入：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内超越基准指数 20%以上； 
增    持：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内超越基准指数 10%-20%； 
中    性：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内相对基准指数变动幅度在-10%-10%之间； 
减    持：预计该公司股价在未来 6-12 个月内相对基准指数跌幅在 10%以上； 
未有评级：因无法获取必要的资料或者其他原因，未能给出明确的投资评级。

行业投资评级： 
强于大市：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现强于基准指数； 
中    性：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现基本与基准指数持平； 
弱于大市：预计该行业指数在未来 6-12 个月内表现弱于基准指数； 
未有评级：因无法获取必要的资料或者其他原因，未能给出明确的投资评级。

沪深市场基准指数为沪深 300 指数；新三板市场基准指数为三板成指或三板做市指数；香港市场基准指数为恒生指数
或恒生中国企业指数；美股市场基准指数为纳斯达克综合指数或标普 500 指数。

### Page 1

삼성바이오로직스 (207940/KS) 
3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 
2026-10-06

제약/바이오. 이선경 / seonkyoung.lee@sks.co.kr / 3773-9089

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 2,000,000 원(유지) 
현재주가: 1,354,000 원 
상승여력: 47.7%

Signal: 파업과 환율 부담에도 컨센 부합 전망  
Key: 5 공장 매출 본격화, 미국 공장 매출 기여에 따른 성장 기대   
Step: 단기 불확실성보다는 중장기적 성장 방향성에 주목

3Q26 Pre: 파업과 환율 부담에도 성장지속, 컨센 부합 전망   
26년 3분기 연결기준 매출액은 1조 3,741억원(+9.3%YoY, +4.0%QoQ), 영업이
익은 6,098억원(-3.7%YoY, +4.0%QoQ, OPM 44.4%)으로 컨센서스 매출액 1
조 3,450억원과 영업이익 6,063억원에 부합하는 실적을 전망한다.  
5월 파업 영향으로 약 20개 배치 생산분(약 1,500억원)에 대한 원가 손실이 인식되
고 3분기 평균 환율이 전분기 대비 약 5% 하락했음에도 불구하고, 3분기부터 매출로 
인식되는 5공장 PPQ 배치 및 미국 공장의 매출 기여로 지속적인 성장이 예상된다. 약 
1,500억원 규모의 생산 차질분은 고객사와 일정 조율을 통해 출하될 예정으로 이에 
따른 4분기 매출 성장을 기대해 볼 수 있다.   
신규 수주 회복과 모달리티 확장에 주목 
시장에서 우려했던 신규 수주는 관세 이슈로 고객사의 컨택이 감소하면서 올해 저조했
으나 4 월 Section 232 결과 발표 이후 논의가 회복 단계에 있어 연내 추가 신규 수주 
가능성은 열려 있다.  
항체 시장의 경쟁심화와 차세대 모달리티 확장에 대응하기 위해 결정한 폴리펩타이드
의 인수는 연내 마무리될 예정이다. 폴리펩타이드의 2028 년 목표 EBITDA 마진은 
25%로 동사 대비 낮은 상황이나, 펩타이드 기반 CDMO 시장 성장에 따른 마진 개선
도 가능하다. 현재 임단협, 환율, 미국 공장 및 폴리펩타이드 인수에 따른 회계 영향 등 
불확실성이 큰 상황이나, 수주 회복과 5 공장 매출 인식 본격화, 미국 공장 및 멀티 모
달리티 확장 등 중장기 성장 방향성에 주목할 필요가 있다.

Document 236: [402340] SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대하이닉스 자사주 소각_31fbe439

Source PDF: all report pdf/[402340] SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대SK스퀘어(402340) 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대하이닉스 자사주 소각_31fbe439.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-09-30
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

SK 스퀘어 (402340/KS) 
하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후가 더 기대 
예정보다 빠른 SK 하이닉스 자사주 매입 
SK스퀘어의 자회사인 SK하이닉스는 지난 8월 20일부터 11월 19일까지 자사주 40
조원(2,407만주, 3.3%)을 매입 예정이다. 9월 29일까지 1,715만주, 예정 매입수량 
대비 71.3%를 매입 완료하였다. 매입 초기 매일 65만주 매입하다가 9월 10일부터는 
60만주를 매입하고 있는데, 이러한 추세가 유지된다면 10월 중순경 자사주 매입이 마
무리될 예정이다. 자사주 매입 후 소각 예정으로 소각이 완료되면 SK스퀘어의 SK하아
닉스에 대한 지분율은 20.0%에서 20.7%로 상승하게 된다.

자사주 매입 이후 추가적인 주주환원에 대한 기대 
SK하이닉스는 지난 8월 19일 정책기간(‘25년~’27년) 동안 발생하는 누적 FCF의 
50% 이상을 주주환원 하는 주주환원 정책을 발표한 바 있다. 26년 SK하이닉스의 예
상 FCF는 164조원(컨센서스 기준)으로 50%를 주주환원 할 경우 자사주 매입 금액
(40 조원)을 제외한 주주환원 재원은 42 조원이다. 잔여 주주환원 재원이 모두 배당으
로 지급된다면 SK 하이닉스 자사주 소각 이후 SK 스퀘어의 지분율은 20.7%이므로 
SK스퀘어의 배당수입도 8.7조원에 달한다. 지난 3월 SK스퀘어는 ‘26년~’28년 경
상배당수입의 30% 이상을 주주에게 환원하는 정책을 발표했다. 보수적으로 30%만 
가정하더라도 SK스퀘어의 배당재원은 2.6조원(현 시가총액 대비 1.8%)이다. SK스퀘
어는 연초 주주총회에서 배당가능이익 확보 차원에서 자본준비금 5 조 8,900억원을 
이익잉여금으로 전입한 바 있다. 이는 26년 재무제표가 확정되는 27년 3월 이후 배당
가능이익으로 편입되므로 SK스퀘어의 주주환원 확대가 기대된다.

투자의견 매수, 목표주가 185 만원(유지) 
SK스퀘어의 순자산 가치 중 98% 이상이 SK하이닉스 지분가치이다. SK하이닉스는 
자사주 매입 이후 17.7% 주가가 상승했으나, SK 스퀘어 주가는 11.4% 상승했다. SK
하이닉스 자사주 매입·소각은 SK 스퀘어의 보유 지분가치 상승뿐 아니라 지분율도 상
승하기 때문에 자사주가 소각될 경우 SK스퀘어의 주가가 탄력적으로 움직일 가능성이 
높다. SK스퀘어 현재 NAV 대비 할인율은 43.7%, 12MF PBR 1.4배로 7월 이후 주
가 조정으로 밸류에이션 매력도 높아졌다. 투자의견 매수와 목표주가 185만원을 유지
한다.

2026-09-30

지주회사/통신서비스/ESG. 최관순 / ks1.choi@sks.co.kr / 3773-8812

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• The recent tendency of some firms to bundle into prices
card-acceptance costs that had previously been surcharged
– and then some, in some cases – demonstrates a ‘cost-
plus’ approach to pricing. To the extent they can pass on
these costs through to prices, it suggests that demand was

strong enough to bear it. The RBA will take this as a signal
that demand is still outstripping supply.
• This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. Base wages
growth has been moderate for some years, though the
above-average increase in award wages this year will add
a little. Not all firms will seek to pass these cost increases
on, but some will, including because they have limited
scope to absorb the cost increase into margins.
• A cost-plus mentality has drawbacks, including that it

limits the imagination away from finding alternative,
efficiency-improving responses. It also accords with
recent academic research suggesting that firms form
expectations and set prices according to what is
happening in their own industry and experience, while
putting little weight on economy-wide inflation or central
bank credibility. This is an unpleasant message for many
consumers or anyone in an interest-sensitive sector when
supply shocks abound.

The recent banning of card surcharges has caused
considerable upset this month, even though it is a reversion
to historical and international practice. The card schemes
used to impose “no-surcharge” rules, until the RBA banned
these in 2003, and many other countries still have them. The
turnaround represents the RBA throwing in the towel on its
longstanding effort to use price incentives to get people to
choose low-cost payment methods. Surcharges no longer

made sense in a largely cashless economy. In any case, least-
cost routing puts the power to choose lower-cost methods in
the hands of the merchants who actually bear the cost.
In most cases, firms will simply roll the surcharge into the sticker
price, leaving the total cost to the consumer unchanged, just
more transparent. We also understand that some large retailers
have absorbed the cost – if they were surcharging in the first
place, which many did not. (The RBA estimated that around

16% of businesses imposed surcharges.) Inevitably, though,
some firms have lifted their prices by more than any reasonable
estimate of the merchant service fee that the surcharge was
meant to cover. And because this is Australia, of course takeaway
coffee has been the centre of consumer discontent.
Higher prices for one’s caffeine fix was unlikely to be the main
instigator of this month’s slump in consumer sentiment. Petrol
‘Cost-Plus’ costs
Forecast changes

• A full update to our forecasts was published in our October
Market Outlook  on Thursday 15 October.
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
prices are typically more salient for cost-of-living concerns, and
the before-and-after picture on the RBA announcement that week
shows that higher interest rates also contributed to the decline.
In some senses, though, the post-surcharge prices and the
RBA’s decisions are linked: businesses passing on increased

costs into their own prices – and expecting to be able to
make that increase stick – is exactly the kind of inflationary
environment the RBA is leaning against. Firms have been
subjected to an array of non-labour cost escalations, from
pandemic-era supply chain issues to electricity to fuel to
water to building supplies. And IT equipment prices, which
for decades were a reliable source of cost reductions, are
nowadays flat to up. Passing these costs on, leaving margins

intact, demonstrates that demand is not weak enough to
prevent this, and signals that firms’ inflationary expectations
might be higher than the RBA desires. The RBA will take this
as a sign that demand is still outstripping supply.
This is mostly a story about non-labour costs. While the Fair
Work Commission did hand down an above-average increase in
award wages this year and this was probably passed through
to prices in hospitality and other labour-intensive sectors, base

wages growth has been moderate for some years. Business
surveys point to non-labour cost escalation being the real issue
lately. This is why we were puzzled by the passage in the RBA
minutes this week suggesting that cost pressures reported
in business surveys might imply that the unemployment rate
consistent with inflation at target (the NAIRU) has risen. It is
a convenient belief, though, if unemployment is starting to
overshoot your forecasts, as the latest labour market data have

done compared with the RBA’s August forecasts.
Not all firms can pass on cost increases. Most small firms do
not have the market power, though coffee may be an exception
because convenience induces a kind of local market power. And
as noted above, large firms with everyday-low-price strategies
will instead try to find savings elsewhere in the supply chain.
Still, some firms will seek to pass these cost increases on.
Some observers might see this as corporate greed, but another

way to frame this strategy is simply as a ‘cost-plus’ mentality.
“Entrenched expectations that cost increases
can and should be passed on are precisely
what tight monetary policy is leaning against,
but only indirectly."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
It turns out that cost-plus pricing is especially common in
areas where regulators determine the price – water and other
utilities being examples where costs and prices have escalated
a lot in recent years.
There are limits to firms’ ability to absorb costs, too. The

failures of so many building firms a few years ago, squeezed
between post-pandemic cost escalation, delivery delays and
fixed-price contracts, were a case in point. Normally, there is
a contractual response, and it need not end in insolvency. The
episode does illustrate, however, that it is unrealistic to expect
firms never to pass cost increases through to prices.
The cost-plus mentality does have its drawbacks. It limits
the imagination away from finding responses that might save

costs elsewhere to absorb the cost hike, and in so doing, boost
efficiency. It was equally limiting in times of soft demand: in
the years before the pandemic, many firms professed a belief
that, because they couldn’t get price increases to stick, they
could not possibly accept cost increases, including in wages.
Thus, Australia ended up with wages growth too low to be
consistent with inflation at target, and the peculiar spectacle
of a central bank governor complaining that wages growth

was too low. And if labour remains cheap, why invest to
economise on it and thereby boost productivity?
A cost-plus mentality also amplifies the pass-through of
supply shocks. This is not quite the same thing as the ‘inflation
expectations’ seen in standard economic models of inflation,
but it is related. The stronger the tendency towards cost-plus
pricing, the worse the inflation response to a supply shock.
This is also true when inflation expectations are high, but the

distinction matters. The recent academic literature includes
some fascinating empirical evidence  from New Zealand that
suggests that beliefs about current and future overall inflation
have little bearing on many firms’ pricing decisions. As related
work using French data shows, firms’ pricing choices are
shaped less by economy-wide inflation developments than by
what is happening to their costs and in their industry.
That adds up to economy-wide behaviour that looks a lot

like firms (in aggregate) setting prices according to expected
inflation (in aggregate), but under the hood something
quite different is going on. If firms’ beliefs about future
economy-wide inflation do not matter much in their pricing
decisions, then neither does the credibility of a central bank’s
commitment to return inflation to target. The mechanism to
get inflation down then must be simply to weaken demand
enough to convince firms that cost-plus will not fly in the

market. This is an unpleasant message for many consumers
or anyone in an interest-sensitive sector when supply shocks
abound. It does, however, imply that if supply shocks fade
or even self-correct, so will high inflation. If and when that
happens, tight monetary policy can be unwound.
The deeper question is whether cost-plus price dynamics can
be reduced. That is more likely to be a question for regulators
than for central banks.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
bias towards an October or December decision. Williams
stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in
this cycle given known information. And Waller also showed
confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are

doing some of the FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest
rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating that
one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation
near the 2.0%yr medium-term target. However, as discussed
in this month’s Market Outlook , capacity pressures are likely
to see inflation consistently above target, rather than below,
in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity in the
housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to

remain modestly restrictive over the forecast horizon and will
also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade
account. At USD133.5bn, the September result was a whisker
inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More
impressively, throughout 2025 and 2026 year-to-date the
surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the
average of 2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth
of export growth is as impressive as the headline annual gain of

36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese
investment in neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time,
both trade and income receipts from offshore investment should
buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound
foreign investment should moderate the pace of gains against
other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the
evolution of producer prices across Asia, and the likelihood

of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent
decades continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was
one of the key drivers of declining goods prices. While
still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing
development to instead be driven by capital, technology and
energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking
production are also increasingly prioritising resilience, security
and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore more

probably future supply-chain developments will help contain
rising costs through productivity than drive sustained price
declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September
Labour Force Survey . Employment surprised to the
downside, –39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force
participation from August’s near-historic high largely offset.
The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August

was revised up.
Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless,
the take home point from the update is that the labour
market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs
below working-age population growth. We expect the
unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter
financial conditions and cost-of-living pressures restrict
activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast
peak is near at 4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the

communication challenge for the RBA. The September minutes
were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing
on materialising upside risks to inflation. But, with the
unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption
of 4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the
labour market is becoming harder to defend. Still, with a clear
focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our
near-term CPI forecasts in the October Market Outlook  – we

still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in
September, taking the annual rate up to 3.7%yr. Energy prices
were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation
was a touch above consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr).
Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months
(0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4% (3.1%yr) on a

0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport
services and a 0.3% increase in medical care services (2.7%yr
and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release
of the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures,
with the headline and core measures up 6.3%yr and 5.0%yr
respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these
developments, firms across multiple districts citing tariffs,
insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost

pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect
further price increases as contracts/hedges roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is
enough signal in these reports to warrant a follow-up hike
at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee
members believe they are near the end of the tightening cycle
and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman
both showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear

Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Downward revisions to 2027 outlook
This week we have updated our New Zealand economic
outlook for the next year, in particular reflecting the latest
developments in the energy market and key financial
variables (long-term interest rates and the exchange rate).

After last week’s marking down of our forecast for through-
year GDP growth in 2026 to 2.5%yr, this week we have
reduced our forecast of growth in 2027 by 0.3ppts to 2.7%yr.
This downward revision reflects a less positive assessment
of the outlooks for consumption and investment (including
residential investment) considering the more elevated path
assumed for energy prices and a higher near-term track for
long-term interest rates (as discussed in our updated Market

Outlook, published earlier this week).
A partial offset to weaker growth in domestic demand is
provided by a slightly more positive outlook for exports
(particularly exports of manufactured goods and tourism
inflows) because of a downward revision to our expectations
for the NZ dollar. We now expect NZD/USD to end this
year around current levels at 0.56 – 3c lower than expected
previously – before appreciating to around 0.64 over the
course of 2027 as interest rate differentials with the US

gradually close and as the USD appreciates. We think that the
low value of the NZ dollar – in trade-weighted terms now at a
17-year low – helps to explain the recent strong performance
in the previously moribund manufacturing sector, as well as
tourist inflows from key markets such as Australia.
Last week we raised our forecast for CPI inflation at year end
to 4.3%yr, largely on account of recent developments in global
energy prices (although the weak NZ dollar has compounded the

effect). This week we have slightly revised up our forecast for
inflation for the first half of 2027. But because energy prices are
assumed to decline in 2027 from what is now a higher starting
level, our 2027 year-end forecast for inflation has nudged
down 0.1ppts to 1.6%yr. Needless to say, forecasts of headline
inflation, in particular, are subject to significant revision given the
uncertain outlook for the Middle East conflict and uncertainty

about how that will be reflected in global energy prices.
Ahead of next week’s very important Q3 CPI report – which
could prove decisive – we have made no changes to our
forecast for the RBNZ’s policy settings. So, for now we
continue to think that despite the higher near-term inflation
outlook the RBNZ will stick with the strategy communicated
in the September Monetary Policy Statement, i.e. that at the
vv28 October meeting it will pause to assess the impact of this

year’s 50bps of policy tightening, before considering a further
upward adjustment in December.
Turning to this week’s data, the news from the housing market
remained decidedly downbeat. House sales fell a further 4% in
September – the seventh consecutive month of decline – to be
12% lower than a year earlier. With the stock of listings at an
11-year high, the median selling time remains elevated. And
so unsurprisingly, house prices fell for a fourth consecutive

month and are now down 1.6%yr. Prices remain especially
soft in the North Island, with prices down more than 3%yr in
Auckland and more than 4%yr in Wellington. Given upward
pressures on mortgage rates, broader strength in the economy
– including a stronger labour market – will likely be required to
reverse this trend. On that score, MBIE’s measure of online job
advertisements was little changed in September, continuing
the flat trend since the beginning of this year.

In other news, retail spending, as measured by electronic card
payments, decreased 0.4%m/m in September following an
unrevised 0.9% decline in August. This was a weaker result than
we had expected based on the recent sawtooth pattern and
our own internal data. We should treat these data with some
suspicion given that for quite a while now the Stats NZ series
seems not to have reflected the spending seen through banks’
own card spending networks, perhaps suggesting changes in the

payments arena may be leaving the official sector data behind.
Even so, unsurprisingly, with petrol prices rebounding late in
the month, spending on fuel increased 2.9%mth. However,
core spending, which excludes spending on fuel and vehicles,
fell 0.7%m/m. While spending on consumables (groceries)
increased modestly in September, this was at the expense of
spending on ‘nice to haves’. Spending on hospitality suffered a
Darren Gibbs
Senior Economist

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
further 2.3%mth fall following a 1.9%mth decline in August,
and spending on apparel replicated the 2.0%mth decline
reported in August. Spending on durable goods also fell
slightly in September (-0.4%mth).
Despite the weak outcome for the August and September

months, retail spending over the September quarter still
eked out a minor 0.1% gain, while core spending increased
0.4%. Given higher prices, at face value this points to a weak
quarter for volumes (next week’s CPI report will cast more
light on pricing behaviour during the quarter). It is worth
noting that total retail spending, as will be depicted in the
official quarterly retail sales survey released next month, could
point to somewhat stronger growth. Purchases of big-ticket

items that often are bought on credit – such as cars, furniture
and appliances – are underrepresented in the electronic card
spending survey.
Given subdued domestic demand, local hospitality businesses
have relied on foreign visitors to drive growth in spending.
And the good news is that visitor arrivals increased to a new
post-Covid high in August to be up 8%yr. Arrivals of Chinese
citizens have grown by almost a third to sit near pre-Covid
levels in recent months, while arrivals of US citizens grew

17%yr. However, the Middle East conflict has impacted
long-haul arrivals from Europe (UK arrivals are down 8%yr).
Meanwhile, inflows of migrants remain at modest levels,
totalling around 20,000 in the year to August – about half
what might be expected on average over the cycle.
News from the manufacturing sector remained positive in
September, with the BusinessNZ Manufacturing PMI rising
to a 3-month high of 56.5 in September. Improvement was
seen across all subcomponents of the index, with the new

orders index rising 1.8pts to 57.0. We think that the recovery in
manufacturing this year owes much to the weakness of the NZ
dollar – especially against the Australian dollar – and activity
associated with a strong domestic primary sector and an
emerging recovery in domestic residential construction activity.
Last but certainly not least, ahead of next week’s all-important
Q3 CPI, the Selected Prices report for September was firmer
than expected. That was mainly due to larger than expected

increases in airfares and holiday accommodation costs, which
offset softer than expected food prices.
Accounting for the September prices data, we’ve left our
forecast for Q3 inflation  at 0.7%. That would see the annual
inflation rate dropping back to 3.7%, down from 4.1% in the
year to June. Underlying that move has been a moderation in
fuel prices after they rocketed higher in the wake of the Middle
East war. That’s balanced against increases in council rates,

building costs and food prices.
Our forecast is lower than the RBNZ’s September MPS
forecast for a 0.8% rise (+3.9%yr). However, with some large
swings in volatile items like fuel, the RBNZ will likely focus
on measures of core inflation. Core inflation was already at
firm levels in the June quarter, with key measures sitting in
the upper part of the RBNZ’s target band. We expect that
they will take another step higher in September, to sit around

3%. Upside surprises in core inflation would be an important
development for the RBNZ ahead of the October OCR review,
as this could suggest that the lower NZD and higher oil prices
are boosting underlying price pressures by more and sooner
than they may have expected.

### Page 7

Q3 is likely to produce a very similar outcome to Q2, with 
quarterly growth of around 1.0% holding the annual rate at 
4.3%yr. Year-to-date, growth will remain around 4.7%ytd 
thanks to the Q1 result.
The detail and partial data for September will again highlight 
the weak state of the consumer and domestic-oriented 
businesses, but also the incredible strength of exports. With 
the trade surplus back at record highs, there are limits to the 
contribution possible from net exports hence, necessitating 
a strengthening of domestic demand if aggregate growth is 
to continue at the rate authorities intend into 2027. Expect 
additional stimulus through the turn of the year.

Document 283: Chartpack _ Advanced economies_ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-10-06)

Report source: BNP Paribas

Pages: 12
Status: ok

### Page 1

By Advanced Economies Team
Advanced Economies:
Resilience to shock after shock
October 2026

### Page 2

• The global economy is in the midst of a growth cycle. As a result, GDP growth is more resilient to shocks than initially
anticipated.
• The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise in oil prices, whose negative impact on growth is offset
by a series of reflationary forces.
• The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial intelligence (AI).
• The world’s major economies are currently engaged in a broader push to expand their capital goods investment.

• While European and US households are experiencing more or less the same energy price shock, inflation and household
consumption remain higher in the US.
• Higher and above-target inflation calls for monetary tightening.
• Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks examined in this publication (the US, the UK, the
Eurozone). Inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027, but there is a significant risk that energy inflation will

spread further to other components of inflation in the future.
• Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength (in the US).
• Monetary policies also have to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal policy (particularly in Japan).
• The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: the ensuing rise in energy prices contributes to the recent increase in
long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the start of the year) to levels close to those of 2002.

• The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period of growth), reflecting different fiscal trajectories. In the
US, the UK, France and Germany the public debt-to-GDP ratio is increasing, while it has stabilized in Italy and decreased in
Spain and Japan.
• The rise in long-term interest rates will weigh on public finances. The fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it will be in 2030.
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with GDP growth.

For more information, please refer to our publication:
EcoPerspectives – Advanced Economies | 3rd quarter of 2026, as of September 24, 2026– Economic Research– BNP Paribas
Advanced economies: Resilience to shock after shock

Document 336: GLOBAL MARKET CALL_ Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight

Source PDF: all report pdf/GLOBAL MARKET CALL_ Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-18
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: GLOBAL MARKET CALL: Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight
Date:    3 hours ago
GLOBAL MARKET CALL: Downgrading 'Go Global' To Market Weight

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
GLOBAL MARKET CALL:
Downgrading 'Go Global' To
Market Weight
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 18 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Robert Nyman / Unsplash
We are downgrading the “Go Global” portfolio strategy from overweight to
market weight and raising “Stay Home” (in the US) from underweight to
market weight.
Overseas earnings remain impressive, but the rallies in emerging market
economies’ stock markets have been led by just a handful of AI-related
stocks. Last week's rout in South Korea showed how vulnerable such
concentration leaves these stock markets. Additionally, European

economies look set to struggle as France's fiscal problems loom, and
China's economy depends almost entirely on exports. Plus, the Middle
East war could flare up again before or after the US midterm elections on
November 3, keeping oil prices, inflation, and interest rates elevated.
Here's more:
I. Global Bonds
The global bond rout took a breather last week. Yields fell in all eight
major bond markets we track, led by the US (chart). The US 10-year

Treasury yield fell 16bps to 5.08%, even as the CPI rose 3.7% y/y and the
PPI jumped 6.3% in September. That suggests bond investors see these
rates as attractive relative to their long-term inflation expectations.
The spread between French and German 10-year yields has stabilized at
around 137bps, near its recent high (chart). Spreads for Italy, Greece,

### Page 3

Spain, and Portugal have eased slightly from their late-September peaks.
Contagion from France has eased for now. But France's own fiscal risk
premium hasn't. The National Assembly began debating France's 2027
budget on October 13. Meanwhile, Paris is burning!
II. Global Stocks
Brazil leads the country ETF rankings mtd, up 12.3% in dollars, ahead of
next Sunday's presidential runoff (chart). South Korea is at the bottom,
down 17.7%. The US is up 0.1%, ahead of the All Country World ex-US

ETF, down 1.9%.

### Page 4

Europe's share of the All Country World MSCI's market cap has fallen to
13.0%, from as high as 30.8% in 2007 (chart). It looks set to keep losing
ground for a while. The US MSCI’s share has firmed to 64.8%, up from
62.4% in March. The Emerging Markets MSCI’s share has held at 12.1%
despite the Korean selloff.
The ratio of the US MSCI to the All Country World ex-US MSCI has turned
up in recent weeks (chart). It remains below its early-2025 high.

### Page 5

III. Earnings & Valuation
The All Country World ex US MSCI's forward earnings is up 41.4% y/y,
while forward revenues is up 11.0% (chart). The global economy is doing
well.
Overseas, earnings have far outpaced stock prices this year. The All
Country World ex-US MSCI is up 10.5% ytd, while its forward earnings is
up 35.9% (chart). Its forward P/E has dropped 18.9% ytd.

### Page 6

The US MSCI's forward P/E is 19.1 versus 12.2 for the All Country World
ex-US, a 57% premium (chart). US profit margins justify about half of it.
The US forward profit margin is 16.8%, about a third higher than the All
Country World ex-US's 12.7%.
IV. China Focus
China remains a source of deflation for the global economy through its
massive trade surplus. Exports jumped 36.3% y/y in September to a
record high (chart). The trade surplus was $133.5 billion. Semiconductor

exports more than doubled during the first nine months of the year.
Booming exports are the only reason China's economy isn't imploding.

### Page 7

The domestic economy remains weak, weighed down by a rapidly aging
population and a property debacle. The Shenzhen Real Estate Stock
Price Index is testing a floor that has held since 2012 (chart). We would
worry if it broke below that level.
The stock market hasn't benefited from the export boom either, so Beijing
appears to be propping it up again. Regulators met on Thursday to
discuss stabilization measures, according to Bloomberg. They reportedly

urged companies and state funds to buy shares and asked some
investors to curb selling. Mainland A-shares, where Beijing's state-backed
funds do most of their buying, held up into mid-2026 before turning down
(chart). Offshore Chinese stocks have been falling since late 2025.

### Page 8

clause.

Document 348: Ken Griffin详解“AI大跌”：算力类似航空，“边际定价”、“折旧期限”与“企业预算”三大陷阱

Source PDF: all report pdf/Ken Griffin详解“AI大跌”：算力类似航空，“边际定价”、“折旧期限”与“企业预算”三大陷阱.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

Citadel 创始⼈ Ken Griffin 认为 AI 算⼒投资有三⼤潜在陷阱： GPU 与航空公司同构，⼀旦供
过于求边际价格将⾃由落体；⽼旧 GPU 的⾼租⾦溢价难以为继，投资机构 7 ⾄ 9 年折旧模型
⾯临重估；企业端 AI ⽀出已从试错转向 ROI 精算、能省则省，与巨头天量资本⽀出形成⽭
盾。
摘要：
10 ⽉ 21 ⽇，在 Citadel 年度全球市场未来峰会上， Griffin 接受彭博记者 Dani Burger 采访时表⽰，
市场正从过去两年的 " 主题式扫货 " 转向真正意义上的基本⾯研究。他说： " 我认为这个市场正在
经历的，是⼈们真正开始做功课。 "
过去两年，市场逻辑简单粗暴：数据中⼼万亿级建设潮开启，买⼊全产业链卡位节点 —— 英伟
达、⾼带宽内存 DRAM 、超微多层陶瓷电容器 MLCC ，以及韩国、中国台湾、⽇本供应商和云计
算巨头 —— ⼏乎稳赚。如今，这套逻辑正在被重新审视。近⽇， AI 交易也遭遇⼤幅重挫，半导体
板块遭遇了⾦融危机以来最⻓连跌。

Ken Griffin 警告： GPU 与航空公司⾼度相似，边际运⾏成本极低，⼀旦算⼒供过于求，现货租赁
与 API 调⽤价格将瞬间崩塌，重演 2000 年电信泡沫中 " 暗光纤 " 的覆辙。
陷阱⼀：边际定价崩塌 ——GPU 的 “ 航空公司时刻 ”
Griffin ⽤航空业打了⼀个直接的⽐⽅。
"GPU 和航空公司⾮常相似，边际运⾏成本极低。航班上有⼀个空座，哪怕只卖 20 美元，那个座
位也是赚钱的。 " 他说， " 正是这⼀特性决定了，⼀旦航空公司出现运⼒过剩，定价就会⾃由落
体。 "
他随即将这⼀逻辑延伸⾄算⼒市场： " 如果我们到达⼀个 GPU 供给超过需求的节点，边际定价实
际上将会⾃由落体。 "
Griffin 援引 2000 年电信泡沫作为历史参照：当年⼤量铺设的光纤，因需求不⾜从未被点亮，成
为 " 暗光纤 " ，电信价格随之直线下跌。他的问题是：算⼒市场是否正⾛向同⼀条路？
" 这将彻底颠覆⼈们今天所依赖的所有投资假设。这是第⼀个问题。 "
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
陷阱⼆：折旧模型失效 ——“ 有⽤寿命 ” 被⾼估
Griffin 提出的第⼆个问题，直指投资机构的估值模型。
" 现在有⼈⽤ 5 ⾄ 6 年前的⽼旧 GPU ，依然能收取极⾼的租⾦。这种情况在未来 5 ⾄ 6 年⾥⼤概率不
会持续。 " 他说， " 因此，越来越多的⼈开始担忧：我不能再像 12 个⽉前那样，对⼀块芯⽚的第
七、⼋、九年给予同样的资本化率或收⼊模型。 "
换⾔之，若投资机构仍按 7 ⾄ 9 年的⾼资产残值进⾏折旧贴现，其估值模型将⾯临系统性重估压
⼒。
陷阱三：企业预算收紧 ——ROI 精算时代来临
Griffin 指出，企业端的 AI ⽀出逻辑已经发⽣根本转变。
" 美国最精明的企业，正在变得⾮常清醒 —— 哪些业务需要为 OpenAI 和 Anthropic 的顶尖模型⽀
付极⾼价格，哪些业务可以⽤开源模型以极低成本运⾏。 "
他补充道： " 如果你看看那些在内部真正⽤好 AI 的公司，它们正在迅速变得⾮常善于最⼤化每⼀
分 AI ⽀出的回报。我认为整个企业界都在看着来⾃ Anthropic 和 OpenAI 的账单，然后说：好，我

们需要想清楚，怎么确保我们真的从中获得了⽣产⼒。 "
7500 亿美元与企业缩减账单的正⾯碰撞
这⼀企业端收紧趋势，与 AI ⼤模型公司的扩张计划形成了直接⽭盾。
据《华尔街⽇报》报道， Anthropic 在其 S-1 ⽂件中披露，未来计划投⼊ 7500 亿美元算⼒开⽀，
且⽀出规模仍在增加。
记者 Dani Burger 直接问 Griffin ：这两件事之间是否存在张⼒？
Griffin 的回答简短⽽直接： " 是的。你在提问的时候就已经回答了这个问题。 "
他随后指出，问题的关键在于 Anthropic 能否将⾃⾝模型打造成 " 必需品 "—— 例如在⽣命科学领
域，若其模型真正能够帮助攻克癌症或其他致命疾病，那么每家制药公司都将不惜任何代价购
买其技术。 " 但我们现在不知道答案。 "
泡沫之问：历史总是相似，但结局未必相同
对于市场上⽇益升温的 "AI 泡沫 " 讨论， Griffin 给出了⼀个有历史纵深的回答。
" 在这个时刻，⼏乎可以肯定存在局部泡沫。任何对经济的根本性变⾰，通常都与泡沫交织在⼀

起。 " 他说， " 我只希望我当年留住了亚⻢逊的股票。 "
他的判断是：泡沫破裂后留下的，往往是改变世界的技术基础设施，⽽穿越泡沫的公司，将成
为未来数⼗年的市场领导者。
但他同时强调，这并不意味着当下的定价⻛险可以被忽视 —— 尤其是在边际定价、折旧假设和
企业预算这三个维度上，市场的重新定价才刚刚开始。

Energy - Kuwait pumping more than 2m b/d
Oil prices remain well bid, with ICE Brent back above $110/bbl this
morning following additional deadly attacks on Saudi Arabia.
Drones struck the Shaybah oilﬁeld and Dammam's international
airport. An oil processing plant was the target of the attack. Saudi
Arabia’s energy ministry said production hasn’t been impacted. The
escalation prompted the US State Department to order non-
emergency US government personnel to leave the country. This will
concern markets, suggesting little improvement in Middle East
tensions, and possibly further escalation.
Despite the volatile environment, oil ﬂows from the Persian Gulf are
holding up. According to the CEO of Kuwait Petroleum Corp, Kuwait

104m barrels, a seasonal low dating back 30 years.
Metals — Indonesia nickel output cuts
LME nickel prices gained signiﬁcantly yesterday, settling 3.65%
higher on coordinated production cuts in Indonesia. Major Chinese
nickel producers operating in Indonesia plan to curb production to

support weak nickel prices. Under the agreement, reﬁneries would
halve production of mixed hydroxide precipitate (MHP), a key
battery feedstock. Nickel pig iron (NPI) output for stainless steel
production would be reduced by about 20%. A similar proposal
failed to reach consensus in August, but persistent price weakness
increased pressure on producers to take action.

company remains on track to fully restore operations by the ﬁrst

Document 408: Top of Mind_ Higher bond yields_ how concerning_

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind_ Higher bond yields_ how concerning_.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-10-13
Pages: 31
Status: ok

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Global bond yields have risen to multi-decade highs, yet growth and markets have
remained remarkably resilient. But with some pressures beginning to emerge (e.g. in
European sovereign bond markets), can this resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities
be revealed? We ask the IMF’s Tobias Adrian, Moody’s Analytics’ Mark Zandi, and GS
economists. Adrian is relatively sanguine about the economy’s resilience, though
notes downside risks, while GS economists expect only a modest growth drag from

higher rates, but a more consequential one if yields remain near current or higher
levels. Zandi is generally more concerned, describing the US economy as vulnerable
rather than resilient, and sees one-in-three odds of the economy going off the rails.
Meanwhile, GS strategists and analysts are not especially concerned about banks or
markets more broadly, but warn that further increases in yields or rate volatility could
test risk assets. Key to watch: whether the increasingly interconnected and leveraged

AI investment boom can continue to withstand higher rates.
Tobias Adrian, Special Advisor and former Director of Monetary and Capital Markets, IMF
“The global economy has demonstrated considerable resilience in the face of multiple severe shocks, and I would not be
surprised if it continues to shrug off higher yields, provided they do not keep rising sharply in a disorderly fashion. At the same
time, I do worry about downside risks.”
Mark Zandi, Chief Economist, Moody’s Analytics

We provide a brief snapshot on the most important economies for the global markets

### Page 3

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Recent acceleration in the rise in global bond yields to multi-
decade highs has begun to raise questions about the 
implications for growth and markets. Both have remained 
remarkably resilient so far, with US growth in particular 
holding relatively firm and the S&P 500 and other equity 
markets near new all-time highs. But with pressure 
beginning to emerge—most notably in European sovereign 
bond markets—and yields looking unlikely to retreat soon, 
can that resilience last, or will more vulnerabilities be revealed? 
GS Head of US Rates Strategy William Marshall first lays out 
how we arrived at higher rates, underscoring the striking 
breadth of the rise, which he attributes mainly to 
fundamentals—strong growth, inflation risks, hawkish policy 
shifts, the ongoing investment cycle, and long-run fiscal 
risks—but notes that technical factors likely amplified the 
recent move. And while he ultimately expects yields to fall 
(with YE 10-year US Treasury yields of 4.75%/4.4% in 
2026/2027, respectively) as markets become more confident 
in easing inflation, he thinks elevated long-term yields should 
have some staying power, and sees continued upside risk 
from energy price and fiscal pressures.  
So, just how much of a threat do higher rates pose to the 
macro outlook? For answers, we first turn to Tobias Adrian, 
Special Advisor to the Managing Director and former 
Financial Counsellor and Director of the Monetary and Capital 
Markets Department at the IMF, Mark Zandi, Chief 
Economist of Moody’s Analytics, and GS US economists. 
Adrian sees a global economy that has so far responded to 
the yield shock much as it has to the many other major 
shocks of the past seven years: resiliently. He notes that the 
rise in yields has not significantly tightened financial 
conditions or, more importantly, disrupted market functioning 
or forced damaging deleveraging. He believes that resilience 
could persist as long as the move in yields doesn’t accelerate 
or become more disorderly. But he is concerned about 
vulnerabilities in the AI and technology complex, sovereign 
bond markets, and EMs—not to mention cyber risks—all of 
which leaves him “vigilant but cautiously optimistic.” 
GS US economists also characterize the US economy as 
resilient. Their expectation that the Fed will deliver just one 
more rate hike this year before cutting next year—consistent 
with Marshall’s expectation of lower Treasury yields—leads 
Pierfrancesco Mei to expect only a modest 0.2pp growth 
drag in 2027 from higher rates. But he finds that the drag 
could rise to a more consequential 0.5pp if yields remain near 
current levels (see pg. 9 for GS global economists Joseph 
Briggs’ and Johan Allen’s rules of thumb for the impact of 
higher yields on rate-sensitive areas across major DMs.) 
David Mericle and Mei also worry about the implications for 
the already-concerning US fiscal trajectory, which they 
believe is unlikely to trigger a classic debt crisis but is too risky 
to ignore.     
Zandi, for his part, is generally more concerned. While his 
baseline outlook is relatively sanguine, he describes the US 
economy as vulnerable rather than resilient and views the 
“distribution of possible outcomes as very flat and heavily 
skewed toward darker tail scenarios.” He sees the clearest 
softness in the labor market, with weak hiring and falling real 
wage growth for many workers weighing on consumer 
spending. And he takes little comfort from US growth 
continuing to run around 2%—roughly its potential rate—
noting that AI accounts for roughly one-third of that growth, 
leaving the non-AI economy growing at only around 1-1.5%, 
which he calls “a pretty punk economy.” Zandi also worries 
that the US fiscal situation is “as bad as I have ever seen it” 
and that higher rates are already inflicting damage on real 
estate markets and squeezing existing debtors. As these 
stresses mount, he sees elevated risk of something breaking 
in the financial system. All told, he puts the odds of the 
current vulnerabilities pushing the economy completely off 
the rails at around one-in-three.  
With banks historically at the center of the rate shocks and 
economic crises that Zandi is worried about, we then explore 
how much risk banks pose today. Adrian and GS Head of the 
Financials Equity Group Richard Ramsden both see little 
cause for alarm, explaining that higher rates should boost 
banks’ net interest income and revenues given their asset-
sensitive balance sheets. Ramsden also doesn’t expect a 
significant deterioration in credit quality absent a broader 
economic downturn and says that most banks should have 
excess capital even after accounting for rate-driven portfolio 
losses.   
But even if banks can weather higher rates, what about 
markets more broadly? GS strategists and analysts are not 
especially concerned, with Chief US Equity Strategist Ben 
Snider arguing that current rate levels shouldn’t prevent 
continued strong US earnings growth and the S&P 500 from 
climbing higher, Chief Credit Strategist Amanda Lynam 
viewing the headwind to corporate credit from higher rates 
as manageable amid the solid growth backdrop, and US 
Financials Analyst Alexander Blostein expecting the recent 
slowdown in private market activity to rebound once rates 
stabilize. GS multi-asset strategists Christian Mueller-
Glissmann and Andrea Ferrario add that higher yields have 
strengthened the strategic case for bonds in a multi-asset 
portfolio, though strong earnings continue to favor equities 
for now. But they all warn that if rate volatility remains 
elevated, and especially if yields continue to rise from here, 
risk assets could face mounting pressures.  
All that said, with the AI boom powering the economy and 
markets, everything may come down to whether higher rates 
will derail it. Snider views this as unlikely given the strong 
balance sheets of the firms investing in AI and the expected 
profitability of these investments, with Lynam also seeing a 
high bar to derail AI-related debt issuance. Adrian also 
maintains a positive baseline for the AI economy but is 
concerned about the circularity of investment, increased 
leverage, and the sustainability of revenue growth in the AI 
complex. And Zandi again seems the most worried, warning 
that “AI could become the part of the economy that breaks” 
if rates remain at current levels for much longer.  
Allison Nathan, Editor  
Email: allison.nathan@gs.com     
Tel: 212-357-7504   
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC

Higher bond yields: how concerning?

### Page 4

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 4 
Top of Mind Issue 152

Mark Zandi is Chief Economist of Moody’s Analytics. Below, he argues that the US economy 
is vulnerable rather than resilient and worries that high interest rates could deepen existing 
pockets of weakness and ultimately cause something to break in the financial system.   
The views stated herein are those of the interviewee and do not necessarily reflect those of Goldman Sachs.
Allison Nathan: Prior to the 
September 2026 Fed rate hike, you 
argued that a hike would do little to 
address supply-driven inflation but 
could damage already weak parts of 
the US economy. Where are you 
seeing the most softness? 
Mark Zandi: The clearest softness is 
in the labor market. Monthly payroll 
growth has averaged around 40,000 over the past year and has 
been essentially zero when excluding the healthcare sector. 
Hiring is moribund, hours worked are very low, and while the 
unemployment rate remains low, that is partly a reflection of 
declining labor force participation. Adjusting for that cyclical 
drop would put underlying unemployment in the mid-4% 
range—above the level consistent with full employment—and I 
expect the headline rate to move toward that level over the 
next 6-18 months. Real wage growth is, at best, flat, and for 
many Americans is actually declining, which is eroding 
purchasing power, particularly for lower- and middle-income 
households, who are increasingly drawing down savings to 
sustain spending. So, while the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household fundamentals point to 
very tenuous growth. 
 While the economy is still growing, the 
underlying labor market and household 
fundamentals point to very tenuous growth.”   
Allison Nathan: What is causing this softness? 
Mark Zandi: Policy choices are at the root of the weakness. 
Tariffs, while more consequential last year, continue to weigh 
on growth and boost inflation. Heavy-handed immigration policy 
is also an underappreciated source of weakness. Many firms 
losing immigrant workers cannot afford to raise wages enough 
to attract native-born workers, so they are raising prices to align 
demand with the supply they can produce with a reduced labor 
force. But the most immediate and significant driver of the 
softness is the Iran war, which has pushed up energy prices, 
further fueling inflation and weighing on growth.  
Allison Nathan: But US real GDP growth remains near 2%—
roughly the economy’s potential rate. Isn’t that a relatively 
healthy growth pace? 
Mark Zandi: Real GDP growth is indeed running at 2% yoy, but 
I estimate that AI-related activity accounts for roughly one-third 
of that, or around 0.6–0.7pp. Roughly half of that comes from AI 
capital spending, net of electronics imports for data centers, 
while the balance reflects wealth effects from the surge in AI-
related stock prices that have contributed to an almost doubling 
in the S&P 500 since ChatGPT debuted four years ago, boosting 
net worth and consumer spending, especially among high-
income households. So, the non-AI economy is growing at only 
around 1%–1.5%. That’s a pretty punk economy, and a difficult 
place to be. 
Allison Nathan: The September SEP indicated that most 
FOMC members expect one more rate hike this year, and 
the market is expecting the same. How damaging could 
further tightening be? 
Mark Zandi: While I expect another hike this year, with the 
economy already barely growing at potential, additional hikes 
could push growth below potential. That would further strain 
the already weak non-AI economy and trigger layoffs, 
significantly raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing negative 
spiral. So, further tightening would be a risky endeavor. 
 With the economy already barely 
growing at potential, additional hikes could 
push growth below potential. That would 
further strain the already weak non-AI 
economy and trigger layoffs, significantly 
raising the likelihood of a self-reinforcing 
negative spiral.”   
Higher rates are already inflicting damage on the economy’s 
most rate-sensitive sectors, particularly housing and 
commercial real estate. Refinancing is in a deep freeze. Existing 
home sales have fallen to levels last seen during the teeth of 
the housing crash and the Global Financial Crisis. Home prices 
are moving sideways on average and outright declining in half 
the country. That could easily create broader problems for 
households, especially for those that bought homes in the last 
years after rates began to rise and have virtually no home 
equity. And while commercial real estate had begun to show 
signs of life, renewed rate increases are once again freezing 
activity and putting pressure on property values.  
Higher rates are also squeezing existing debtors as borrowing 
costs on credit cards, home loans, auto loans, and corporate 
debt rise. I am particularly worried about highly leveraged, often 
private equity-backed companies that have managed the higher 
rate environment by extending maturities and limiting near-term 
interest payments. That strategy cannot continue indefinitely if 
borrowing costs remain elevated or continue to rise.  
The equity market is another key area of concern. AI continues 
to propel the equity market, but it is not impervious to higher 
rates. Persistently elevated rates will eventually weigh on 
valuations, increasing the risk of a major correction that could 
quickly reverberate through the real economy as positive wealth 
effects reverse and consumer spending weakens. AI capex is 
also vulnerable as a higher cost of capital makes investment 
Interview with Mark Zandi

### Page 5

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5 
Top of Mind Issue 152

more expensive and falling valuations make it harder to raise 
the capital necessary to sustain the current pace of the AI 
buildout. For now, AI is operating under its own momentum, 
supported by considerable optimism and euphoria. But if rates 
remain at these levels or higher for longer than a quarter or two, 
AI could become the part of the economy that breaks. 
Last but not least, higher rates will worsen the US fiscal 
situation, which is itself contributing to elevated yields today. 
While we have navigated this fiscal risk for some time, the 
fiscal situation is as bad as I have ever seen it. The deficit 
exceeds 6% of GDP, the primary deficit is above 3%, and debt 
is roughly 100% of GDP and on track to reach 125% in a 
decade. Annual federal interest payments are already well 
above $1tn and will rise rapidly if rates remain elevated. All told, 
I see a roughly one-in-five chance of a fiscal cliff event over the 
coming year—unlikely, but large enough to warrant attention. 
So, higher rates will weigh on the economy through several 
channels. Historically, rate increases of this magnitude have 
almost invariably caused something to break in the financial 
system. We cannot know exactly where the fault line will 
emerge, but the risk of these pockets of weakness becoming a 
broader problem is significant. 
 I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed 
toward darker tail scenarios.”  
Allison Nathan: How do these risks translate into your 
forecasts for economic growth? 
Mark Zandi: My baseline forecast is relatively sanguine based 
on the assumption that the Iran war begins to wind down, 
allowing oil prices to decline to around $80/bbl as we move into 
next year. That should ease inflation, reduce Fed tightening 
concerns, and bring the 10-year Treasury yield back toward 
4.5% by early next year—a level the economy can digest.  
Importantly, though, I view the distribution of possible 
outcomes as very flat and heavily skewed toward darker tail 
scenarios. The war may not fade into the background, keeping 
energy prices elevated and raising the risk of further inflation 
pressure. That could prompt additional Fed tightening, push 
yields even higher, and ultimately cause something to break in 
the financial system.  
Meanwhile, the Treasury market has grown more fragile as 
traditional, price-insensitive buyers—including the Fed, banks, 
and foreign institutional investors—have stepped back, 
increasing the market’s reliance on highly leveraged, price-
sensitive hedge funds that could rush for the exits under stress. 
A debt-limit confrontation under a likely divided US government 
next year could provide the catalyst, particularly if lawmakers 
underestimate the consequences of a breach. In that scenario, 
an even sharper bond market selloff than we are currently 
experiencing could occur. While none of this is my baseline—
and I remain inherently optimistic because avoiding the darkest 
scenarios is largely a policy choice—I put the odds of a severe 
bond market selloff at around one-in-four and of the economy 
going completely off the rails for any of the reasons we have 
discussed at around one-in-three. So, the downside risks are 
significant. 
 I put the odds of a severe bond market 
selloff at around one-in-four and of the 
economy going completely off the rails for 
any of the reasons we have discussed at 
around one-in-three. So, the downside risks 
are significant.”  
Allison Nathan: The Fed has considerable room to cut 
rates. Should that provide any comfort that the worst-case 
scenarios may be avoided? 
Mark Zandi: That remains an open question. If inflation fades 
and allows the Fed to take its foot off the brake, rate cuts 
should bring yields down across the curve—though not back to 
pre-war levels. But in a darker scenario involving a debt limit 
crisis and even higher yields, short-term rate cuts would have 
little effect on the long end. The Fed would need to turn to 
alternative policy tools, including quantitative easing and bond 
purchases. That would amount to financial repression—a 
strategy similar to the one employed by the BoJ for many 
years—which could prove counterproductive by reducing the 
pressure on lawmakers to address the underlying problems 
while fueling even more persistent inflation. 
Allison Nathan: Why do you think others are more 
sanguine about the risks that higher interest rates pose to 
the US economy? 
Mark Zandi: Some likely see greater underlying economic 
strength and expect AI to drive much stronger growth. Others 
may believe that the neutral rate is higher, perhaps for the 
same reasons, while some may be more concerned about 
persistently higher inflation.  
But the more fundamental difference is the broader lens 
through which I currently view the economy. Many continue to 
describe the US economy as resilient, which I find fascinating, 
bordering on frustrating. In my view, the right word is 
vulnerable. Again, growth is pedestrian, the labor market is soft, 
which was further underscored by the much weaker-than- 
expected September jobs report, real wage growth is 
decelerating, inflation remains elevated, and interest rates are 
rising. That shift in perspective completely changes how one 
evaluates the risks ahead.
